\left|\left|\Phi^{\delta,i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}-\Phi^{K,i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right|\right|_{C^{1}\left(\overline{I}^{i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right)}<\delta_{0}.

Now, we recall the definition of the effective potential energy associated to a timelike future-directed geodesic moving in the exterior region of a stationary and axisymmetric spacetime with metric given by (
3.11
):

E_{\ell_{z}}(W,X,\sigma,\rho,z):=\frac{-W(\rho,z)}{X(\rho,z)}\ell_{z}+\frac{\sigma}{X(\rho,z)}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X(\rho,z)}.

We rewrite
E_{\ell_{z}}
in terms of the renormalised unknowns
\displaystyle h:=(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{\sigma},\overset{\circ}{X})
:

E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\rho,z):=-\overset{\circ}{\Theta}\ell_{z}-W_{K}X_{K}^{-1}\ell_{z}+\frac{\rho}{X_{K}}\frac{\overset{\circ}{\sigma}+1}{\overset{\circ}{X}+1}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(\overset{\circ}{X}+1)}

(7.2)

Henceforth,
E_{\ell_{z}}
is seen as a function defined on
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}\times\mathscr{B}
. Moreover, we recall that the set of solutions to the equation

E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\rho,z)=\varepsilon

is called the zero velocity curve
Z(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
. The latter can also be seen as the level curve of
E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot)
at
\varepsilon
.
In the following, we show that when
E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot)
and
E^{K}_{\ell_{z}}
are similar, that is when
h
is small, then their level sets at a level
\varepsilon
, such that
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{A}_{bound}
have the same shape. More precisely, we state the following main result

Proposition 20
.

Let
\tilde{\delta}_{0}>0
. Then there exists
0<\delta_{0}\leq\tilde{\delta}_{0}
such that
\forall h:=\left(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{\sigma},\overset{\circ}{X}\right)\in B(0,\delta_{0})\subset\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}
,
\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
, there exists a unique curve
Z(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\subset\mathscr{B}
solution to the equation

E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\rho,z)=\varepsilon

(7.3)

Moreover, the one-parameter family of solutions
(Z(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))_{||h||\in[0,\delta_{0}[}
is a
\delta_{0}-
perturbation of
Z^{K}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
in the sense of Definition
26
.

In the case of Kerr, i.e.
h=0
, the set of solutions to the equation (
7.3
) cannot be written globally as the graph of a unique function depending on
\rho
or on
z
, since the implicit function theorem cannot be applied globally to (
7.3
) in order to write
\rho
in terms of
z
or
z
in terms of
\rho
. This must be taken into account when defining the space of solutions. In order to overcome this technical difficulty, we have decomposed
\mathscr{B}
into several regions and we have used the reparameterization of
Z^{K}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
so that the implicit function theorem can be applied in each region to solve (
7.3
) with
h=0
. We will now apply the fixed point theorem on each region in order to solve (
7.3
) with small
h
. The set of solutions to the latter is the graph of some function. Finally, we will obtain the set of solutions in the whole region
\mathscr{B}
by gluing all the graphs that we have obtained.

The remaining of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
20
.

7.1
Proof of Proposition
20

Let
\delta_{0}>0
, let
h\in B_{\delta_{0}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}
where
B_{\delta_{0}}
is the open ball of radius
\delta_{0}
centred around
0
and let
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
. The problem of finding solutions in
\mathscr{B}
is equivalent to that of solving the above equation in
\mathscr{B}_{i}
and
\mathscr{B}^{abs}
. Hence, we start with solving (
7.3
) on
\mathscr{B}_{1}
. We state the following lemma

Lemma 48
.

There exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that
\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}}
, there exists a unique function
\Phi^{h,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}:\overline{I}^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that the set of solutions to (
7.3
) on
\mathscr{B}_{1}
is given by
\displaystyle\text{Gr}\left(\Phi^{h,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right)
. Moreover, we have

•

\Phi^{h,1}
is smooth with respect to
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.

•

\Phi^{h,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}
is continuously Fréchet differentiable with respect to
h
,

•

\Phi^{h,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}
is
C^{1}
on
I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
and satisfies

\left|\left|\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}-\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}\right|\right|_{C^{1}\left(\overline{I}^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right)}<\delta_{0}.

(7.4)

Proof.

Let
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
and recall the definition of
\displaystyle\overline{I}^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
. Define the mapping
F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}
by

F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}\left(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{\sigma},\overset{\circ}{X},z\right)=-\overset{\circ}{\Theta}\ell_{z}-W_{K}X_{K}^{-1}\ell_{z}+\frac{\rho}{X_{K}}\frac{\overset{\circ}{\sigma}+1}{\overset{\circ}{X}+1}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(\overset{\circ}{X}+1)}-\varepsilon

on the domain
\displaystyle B_{\tilde{\delta}_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}
, where
B_{\tilde{\delta}_{0}}
is the ball centred at
(0,0,0)
with radius
\tilde{\delta}_{0}
of the product space
\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}
.

•

Existence

1.

It is easy to see that
\forall\rho\in\overline{I}^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})},
the point
(h_{0}:=(0,0,0),z_{0}:=\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho))
is a zero for
\displaystyle F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}
.

2.

Since
(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{\sigma},\overset{\circ}{X})\in C^{2}(\mathscr{B})
,
F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}
is continuously differentiable with respect to
z
on
\mathbb{R}
.

3.

We compute:

\frac{\partial F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}}{\partial z}(h_{0},z_{0})=\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}}{\partial z}(h_{0},\rho,z_{0}).

The latter vanishes if and only if
z_{0}=0
, which is not the case. Therefore,

\phi(\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho):=\left(\frac{\partial F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}}{\partial z}(h_{0},z_{0})\right)^{-1}

is well-defined.

4.

We consider the mapping
F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}
defined on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
by

F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z):=z-\phi(\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho)F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(h,z).

We will show that after shrinking
\tilde{\delta}_{0}
uniformly in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
and
\rho
, that
\forall h\in B(h_{0},\delta_{0})
,
F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}
is a contraction on
\overline{B}(z_{0},\delta_{0})
.

First, by compactness of
\mathcal{B}_{bound}
and
I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
, there exists
C>0
such that
\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
,
\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
:

\left|\phi(\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho)\right|\leq C.

Now, let
h\in B(h_{0},\tilde{\delta}_{0})
and
z\in B(z_{0},\tilde{\delta}_{0})
. We compute

\frac{\partial F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}}{\partial z}(z)=1-\phi(\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho)\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}}{\partial z}(h,\rho,z).

Next, we show that there exists
C
independent of
((\varepsilon,\ell_{z});\rho)
such that
\forall(h,z)\in B_{\tilde{\delta}_{0}}(h_{0})\times B_{\tilde{\delta}_{0}}(z_{0})

\left|\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(h,z)-\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(h_{0},z_{0})\right|\leq C\tilde{\delta}_{0}.

We have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(h,z)-\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(h_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}E_{\ell_{z}}(\rho,z)-\partial_{z}E^{K}_{\ell_{z}}(\rho,z_{0})

\displaystyle-\partial_{z}\overset{\circ}{\Theta}(\rho,z)\ell_{z}-\partial_{z}(W_{K}X_{K}^{-1})(\rho,z)\ell_{z}

\displaystyle+\partial_{z}\left(\frac{\rho(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}+1)}{X_{K}(\overset{\circ}{X}+1)}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(\overset{\circ}{X}+1)}\right)(\rho,z)

\displaystyle+\partial_{z}(W_{K}X_{K}^{-1})(\rho,z_{0})\ell_{z}-\partial_{z}\left(\frac{\rho}{X_{K}}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}}\right)(\rho,z_{0})

\displaystyle=I+II+III,

where

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle:=-\partial_{z}\overset{\circ}{\Theta}(\rho,z)\ell_{z},

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle:=\ell_{z}\left(-(W_{K}X_{K}^{-1})(\rho,z)+(W_{K}X_{K}^{-1})(\rho,z_{0})\right),

\displaystyle III

\displaystyle:=\partial_{z}\left(\frac{\rho(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}+1)}{X_{K}(\overset{\circ}{X}+1)}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(\overset{\circ}{X}+1)}\right)(\rho,z)-\partial_{z}\left(\frac{\rho}{X_{K}}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}}\right)(\rho,z_{0}).

Since ,
\ell_{z}\in[\ell_{1},\ell_{2}]
and
\overset{\circ}{\Theta}\in B_{\tilde{\delta}_{0}}(\mathcal{L}_{\Theta})
, there exists
C>0
uniform in
\rho
,
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
such that

|I|\leq C\tilde{\delta}_{0}.

Concerning the second term, we write:

\displaystyle|II|

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{2}\left|(W_{K}X_{K}^{-1})(\rho,z)-(W_{K}X_{K}^{-1})(\rho,z_{0})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{2}\left(\sup_{\overline{z}\in(z,z_{0})}\left|\partial{z}(W_{K}X_{K}^{-1})(\rho,\overline{z})\right|\right)\left|z-z_{0}\right|.

By
4
,
\displaystyle W_{K}X_{K}^{-1}(\rho,z):=\frac{2dr(\rho,z)}{\Pi(\rho,z)}
is smooth and bounded (with its derivatives) on
\mathscr{B}
. Therefore, there exists
C>0
independent of
(\rho,z)
and
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
so that

|II|\leq C\tilde{\delta}_{0}.

As for
III
, we set

A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(x,y)):=\frac{\rho}{X_{K}}\frac{x+1}{y+1}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(y+1)}\quad\text{where}\quad(x,y)\in[-\overline{\delta}_{0},\overline{\delta}_{0}]^{2}.

A_{\ell_{z}}
is smooth on
\mathscr{B}_{1}\times]-\overline{\delta}_{0},\overline{\delta}_{0}[^{2}
and we have

\nabla_{(\rho,z)}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(x,y))=\frac{x+1}{y+1}\left(\nabla_{(\rho,z)}\left(\frac{\rho}{X_{K}}\right)\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(y+1)}+\frac{\rho(x+1)}{X_{K}}\frac{\nabla_{(\rho,z)}X_{K}}{\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(y+1)}}\right)

and

\nabla_{(x,y)}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(x,y))=\frac{\rho}{X_{K}}\left(\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{y+1}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(y+1)}\\
&-\frac{(x+1)}{(y+1)^{2}}\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(y+1)}+\frac{x+1}{y+1}\frac{X_{K}}{2\sqrt{\ell_{z}^{2}+X_{K}(y+1)}}\end{aligned}\right).

The functions
\displaystyle(\rho,z)\to\frac{\rho}{X_{K}}
and
\displaystyle(\rho,z)\to X_{K}(\rho,z)
are bounded on
\mathscr{B}_{1}=\mathscr{B}_{1}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
and by compactness of
\mathcal{B}_{bound}
, the latter are bounded independently from
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.

Therefore, there exists
C>0
uniform in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
such that
\forall(\rho,z)\in\mathscr{B}_{1}\,,\,\forall(x,y)\in]-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}[^{2}

\left|\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(x,y))\right|\leq C\overline{\delta}_{0}.

Now, we have

\displaystyle III

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}\left(A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)))\right)-\partial_{z}\left(A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(0,0))\right)(\rho,z_{0})

(7.5)

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)))+\partial_{z}\overset{\circ}{\sigma}\partial_{x}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)))

\displaystyle+\partial_{z}\overset{\circ}{X}\partial_{y}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)))-\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z_{0}),(0,0))

Since
\overset{\circ}{\sigma}\in B_{\overline{\delta}_{0}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{X}
and
\overset{\circ}{X}\in B_{\overline{\delta}_{0}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{X}
, the functions
\overset{\circ}{X}
and
\sigma_{0}
are bounded (with their derivatives) on
\overline{\mathscr{B}}
. In particular, we have

\forall(\rho,z)\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\;,\;|\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z)|\,,\,|\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)|\,,\,\left|\partial_{z}\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z)\right|\,,\,\left|\partial_{z}\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)\right|\leq\overline{\delta}_{0}.

Moreover,

\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)))-\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z_{0}),(0,0))=\text{(a)}+\text{(b)}

where

\text{(a)}:=\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)))-\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z_{0}),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z_{0}),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z_{0})))

and

\text{(b)}:=\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z_{0}),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z_{0}),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z_{0})))-\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z_{0}),(0,0))

We have
\forall z\in B(z_{0},\overline{\delta}_{0})
,

(a)

\displaystyle:=(z-z_{0})\partial_{z}\left(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z)\;\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z)\right)\cdot\nabla_{(x,y)}\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}\left((\rho,z_{0}),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z_{0}),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z_{0}))\right)

\displaystyle+(z-z_{0})\partial_{zz}A_{\ell_{z}}\left((\rho,z_{0}),(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z_{0}),\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z_{0}))\right)+O((\overline{\delta}_{0})^{2})

By similar arguments to the first estimates, we can find
C>0
uniform in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
such that
\forall(\rho,z)
we have

|\text{(a)}|\leq C\overline{\delta}_{0}.

In order to estimate (b), we write

\text{(b)}=\left(\overset{\circ}{\sigma}(\rho,z_{0})\;\overset{\circ}{X}(\rho,z_{0})\right)\cdot\nabla_{(x,y)}\partial_{z}A_{\ell_{z}}((\rho,z_{0}),(0,0))+O((\overline{\delta}_{0})^{2}).

Again by similar arguments as above, we can find
C>0
uniform in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
such that
\forall(\rho,z)
we have

|\text{(b)}|\leq C\overline{\delta}_{0}.

Finally, after shrinking
\overline{\delta}_{0}
, there exists
C>0
such that

|III|\leq C\delta_{0}

Hence, we choose
\tilde{\delta}_{0}
so that

\left|\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z)-\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h_{0}}(z_{0})\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Since,

\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h_{0}}(z_{0})=0,

(7.6)

we are left with a bound on the derivative of
F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}
with respect to
z
:

\left|\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}.

(7.7)

5.

We claim that there exists
\delta_{0}\leq\overline{\delta}_{0}
such that

\forall z\in B\left(\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho),\overline{\delta}_{0}\right)\;:\quad\left|F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z)-\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Indeed,
\displaystyle\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}\;,\;\displaystyle\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\;,\;\forall h\in B(h_{0},\overline{\delta}_{0})
and
\displaystyle\forall z\in B(\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho),\overline{\delta}_{0})

\displaystyle F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z)-\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho)

\displaystyle=F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z)-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h_{0}}(z_{0}=\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho))

\displaystyle=F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z)-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z_{0})+F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z_{0})-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h_{0}}(z_{0})

By the mean value theorem,

\left|F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z)-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z_{0})\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|z-z_{0}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\overline{\delta}_{0}.

Moreover, by similar estimates to those performed in (
7.5
), there exists
C>0
uniform in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
and
z
such that

\left|F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z_{0})-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h_{0}}(z_{0})\right|\leq C\overline{\delta}_{0}.

Therefore, we choose
\delta_{0}\leq\overline{\delta}_{0}
so that

\left|F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(z)-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h_{0}}(z_{0})\right|\leq\delta_{0}.

By (
7.7
),
\displaystyle F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}_{h}(\rho)
is a contraction on
\overline{B}(\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(z),\delta_{0})
.

6.

By the fixed point theorem, there exists
\delta_{0}\leq\tilde{\delta}_{0}
such that
\displaystyle\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}\;,\;\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\;,\;
there exists a mapping
\Phi^{\cdot.,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho):B(0,\delta_{0})\mapsto B(\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho),\delta_{0})
such that
\displaystyle\Phi^{h,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho)\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X})
and satisfies

F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(h,\Phi^{h,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho))=0.

•

Regularity:

–

Regularity with respect to
h
:
we prove that
h\to\Phi^{h,1}
is
C^{1}
with respect to
h
. For this, let
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
and
\rho\in I_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}^{1}
. In order to lighten the expressions, we will not write the dependence of
\Phi^{h,1}
on
((\varepsilon,\ell_{z});\rho)
and also sometimes write
\Phi^{h,1}
as
\Phi^{1}(h)
.

First, we show that
\Phi^{1}
is Lipschitz. For this, let
h,\overline{h}\in B_{\delta_{0}}
and set

z=\Phi^{1}(h),\quad\quad\overline{z}=\Phi^{1}(\overline{h}).

We have

\displaystyle z-\overline{z}

\displaystyle=\Phi^{1}(h)-\Phi^{1}(\overline{h})

\displaystyle=F_{h}(z)-F_{\overline{h}}(\overline{z})

\displaystyle=F_{h}(z)-F_{h}(\overline{z})+F_{h}(\overline{z})-F_{\overline{h}}(\overline{z})

\displaystyle=F_{h}(z)-F_{h}(\overline{z})+\phi(\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho)\left(F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),z}(h,\overline{z})-F^{\varepsilon,\ell_{z},z}(\overline{h},\overline{z})\right).

We have

\left|F_{h}(z)-F_{h}(\overline{z})\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|z-\overline{z}\right|

and

\displaystyle\left|\phi(\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho)\left(F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(h,\overline{z})-F^{\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho}(\overline{h},\overline{z})\right)\right|\leq C\left|\left|h-\overline{h}\right|\right|_{\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}}.

Therefore,

|z-\overline{z}|\leq 2C\left|\left|h-\overline{h}\right|\right|_{\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}}.

Thus,
\Phi^{1}
is Lipschitz, so continuous on
B_{\delta_{0}}
. Since

\forall\Phi^{1}(\rho)\in B(\Phi^{K,1}(\rho),\delta_{0}),\;\quad\left|\frac{\partial F_{h}^{\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho}}{\partial z}(z)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2},

Hence,

\forall z\in B(\Phi^{K,1}(\rho),\delta_{0}),\,\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}},\quad\quad\frac{\partial F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}}{\partial z}(h,z)\neq 0.

Since
F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}
is differentiable at
(\overline{h},\overline{z})
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(h,z)-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(\overline{h},\overline{z})

\displaystyle=D_{h}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(\overline{h},\overline{z})\cdot(h-\overline{h})+\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(\overline{h},\overline{z})(z-\overline{z})+o\left(||h-\overline{h}||_{\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}}+|z-\overline{z}|\right).

By the above estimates we have

o(|z-\overline{z}|=O(||h-\overline{h}||_{\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}}).

Therefore,

z-\overline{z}=-\left(\partial_{\rho}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(\overline{h},\overline{z})\right)^{-1}D_{h}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}(\overline{h},\overline{z})\cdot(h-\overline{h})+o(||h-\overline{h}||).

–

Regularity with respect to
z
:
Let
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
and let
h\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X})
.

First we show that
\Phi^{h,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
is Lipschitz on
I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
. In order to lighten the expressions, we drop the dependence in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
and
h
so that
\Phi^{h,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
will be denoted by
\Phi^{1}
. Similarly,
\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
will be denoted by
\Phi^{K,1}
. Now, let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
.

We will express
\displaystyle\Phi^{1}(\rho_{1})-\Phi^{1}(\rho_{2})
in terms of
\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}
. To this end, we write,

\displaystyle z_{1}-z_{2}

\displaystyle:=\Phi^{1}(\rho_{1})-\Phi^{1}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{1})-F^{\rho_{2}}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{1})-F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{2})+F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{2})-F^{\rho_{2}}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{1})-F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{2})+\phi(\rho_{1})\left(F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{2})-F^{\rho_{2}}(z_{2})\right)+\left(\phi(\rho_{1})-\phi(\rho_{2})\right)F^{\rho_{2}}(z_{2}).

Again, we have

\left|F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{1})-F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{2})\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\left|z_{1}-z_{2}\right|.

We have

\phi(z_{i})=\left(\frac{\partial F^{\rho_{i}}}{\partial z}(0,\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{i}))\right)^{-1}=\left(\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{i},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{i}))\right)^{-1}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\phi(\rho_{1})-\phi(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{1},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{1}))\right)^{-1}-\left(\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{2},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{2}))\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{1},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{1}))\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{2},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{2}))\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{2},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{2}))-\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{1},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{1}))\right).

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{1},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{1}))-\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{2},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{2}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{\mathscr{B}_{1}}\left|\right|\nabla^{2}E_{\ell_{z}}(\rho,z)\left|\right|\,|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq C|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}|,

where
C
is independent of
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.
We also have

\left(\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{1},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{1}))\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}^{K}}{\partial z}(\rho_{2},\Phi^{K,1}(\rho_{2}))\right)^{-1}\leq C,

for some
C
uniform in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
. Moreover,

\displaystyle F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{2})-F^{\rho_{2}}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\rho_{1},z_{2})-E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\rho_{2},z_{2}).

Since
E_{\ell_{z}}(h,,\cdot,z_{2})
is differentiable on
I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|F^{\rho_{1}}(z_{2})-F^{\rho_{2}}(z_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathscr{B}_{1}}\left|\nabla_{\rho,z}E_{\ell_{z}}(\rho,z)\right|\left|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\right|,

where
C>0
is some constant independent of
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
. Therefore, there exists a constant independent of
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
and
h
such that

\left|\Phi^{1}(\rho_{1})-\Phi^{1}(\rho_{2})\right|\leq C|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}|.

(7.8)

Hence,
\Phi^{1}
is Lipschitz on
I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
. It remains to show that
\Phi^{1}
is continuously differentiable on
I^{1}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.

Now, recall that

\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\in\forall z\in B(\Phi^{K,1}(\rho),\delta_{0}),\,\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}},\quad\quad\frac{\partial F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho}}{\partial z}(h,z)\neq 0.

Since,
(\rho,z)\to F^{\rho}(z)
is differentiable on
\mathscr{B}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{1}}(h,z_{1})-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{2}}(h,z_{2})

\displaystyle=F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{1}}(h,z_{1})-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{2}}(h,z_{1})+F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{2}}(h,z_{1})-F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{2}}(h,z_{2})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\rho}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{1}}(h,z_{1})(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2})+\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{2}}(h,z_{2})(z_{1}-z_{2})+o\left(|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}|+|z_{1}-z_{2}|\right).

By (
7.8
), we have

o(|z_{1}-z_{2}|)=O(|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}|).

Hence,

\displaystyle\Phi^{1}(\rho_{1})-\Phi^{1}(\rho_{2})

\displaystyle=-\partial_{z}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{2}}(h,z_{2})^{-1}\partial_{\rho}F^{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{1}}(h,z_{1})(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2})+O(|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}|)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial_{\rho}E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\rho_{1},\Phi^{1}(\rho_{2}))}{\partial_{z}E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\rho_{2},\Phi^{1}(\rho_{2}))}(\rho_{1}-\rho_{2})+O\left(|\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}|\right).

–

Regularity with respect to
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
:
We claim that
\Phi^{1}
is continuously differentiable on
\mathcal{B}_{bound}
. The regularity in this case is proven in the the same manner.

•

Uniqueness:
We show, after possibly shrinking
\delta_{0}
, that
\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}}\,,\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}\,,\,\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\;
there are no others solutions to the equation (
7.3
) in
[\overline{z}_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\infty[
.
First, we set

\forall z\in[\overline{z}_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),z_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})]\;,\quad P^{h,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z):=E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\rho,z)-\varepsilon.

and

\forall z\in[\overline{z}_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),z_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})];,\quad P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z):=E^{K}_{\ell_{z}}(\rho,z)-\varepsilon.

We claim that for
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small, there exists
\displaystyle C>0
such that
\displaystyle\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}\,,\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\,
,
z\in J:=[\overline{z}_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\infty[\backslash B(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho),\delta_{0})
we have

\left|P^{K,\lx@nobreakspace\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho)\right|>C\delta_{0}.

P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}
is monotonically increasing on
]\overline{z}_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\infty[
. Therefore,
\displaystyle\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}\,
,
\displaystyle\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\,
,
\displaystyle\forall z\in J
:

\left|P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)\right|>\max\left\{\left|P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho)-\delta_{0})\right|,\left|P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho)+\delta_{0})\right|\right\}.

Now, we show that for all
|h|
small, there exist
C(h)>0
uniform in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
such that

|P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho)+h)|>C(h).

We have

P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho)+h)=P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho))+h(P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}})^{\prime}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho))+o(h),

where
o(h)
is uniform in
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
by continuity of
\displaystyle(P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}})^{(k)}
with respect to
((\varepsilon,\ell_{z});\rho)
, compactness of
\mathcal{B}_{bound}
and the fact that
z\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\subset K
where
K
is some compact independent from
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
. Moreover,
\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho)
is a simple root in the sense that
(P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}})^{\prime}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho))=\frac{\partial E_{\ell_{z}}}{\partial z}(\rho,\Phi^{K,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho))\neq 0
and
\displaystyle(\varepsilon,\ell_{z},\rho)\mapsto(P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}})^{\prime}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z},d}(\rho))
is continuous on
\mathcal{B}_{bound}\times K
. Hence, for
h
sufficiently small, we obtain

\left|P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}\left(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho)+h\right)\right|>|h|\left|(P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}})^{\prime}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho))\right|>C|h|,

where
C
is some constant which is uniform in
\delta_{0}
and
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
. Therefore, we update
\delta_{0}
so that

\left|P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)\pm\delta_{0})\right|>C\delta_{0}.

Now, let
\delta_{1}<\delta_{0}
. By uniqueness in the fixed point theorem,
\displaystyle\forall h\in B_{\delta_{1}}\subset B_{\delta_{0}}
,
\displaystyle\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound},

\displaystyle\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\,

\;\Phi^{h,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho)
is the unique solution in the ball
B(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho),\delta_{0})
. Moreover,
\forall z\in J
, we have

P^{h,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)=P^{h,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)-P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)+P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z).

By the triangular inequality, the latter implies for
\left|P^{h,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)-P^{K,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)\right|<\tilde{C}\delta_{1}<C\delta_{0}
that

\forall z\in J,\quad\quad|P^{h,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)|>-\tilde{C}\delta_{1}+C\delta_{0}>0.

Therefore, after updating
\delta_{1}>0
,
P^{h,\rho}_{\varepsilon,\ell}
does not vanish outside the ball
B(\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho),\delta_{1})
. This yields the uniqueness.

Thus, we update
\delta_{0}
in order to obtain that that
\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}}\,,\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}\,,\,\forall\rho\in I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\;
there exist a unique solution to the equation (
7.3
) in the region
[\overline{z}_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\infty[
.
∎

In the same way, we prove the following lemmas:

Lemma 49
.

Let
i=2,\cdots,4
. There exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that
\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}
, there exists a unique
\Phi^{h,i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}:I^{i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that the set of solutions to (
7.3
) on
\mathscr{B}_{i}
is given by
\displaystyle\text{Gr}\left(\Phi^{h,i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right)
. Moreover, we have

•

\Phi^{h,i}
is smooth with respect to
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.

•

\Phi^{h,i}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}
is continuously Fréchet differentiable with respect to
h
,

•

\Phi^{h,i}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}
is
C^{1}
on
I^{i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
and satisfies

\left|\left|\Phi^{i}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}-\Phi^{K,i}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}\right|\right|_{C^{1}\left(\overline{I}^{i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right)}<\delta_{0}.

(7.9)

Lemma 50
.

There exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that
\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
,
\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}
,
\forall K\subset\subset]-\beta,\beta[
,
\forall z\in K
, there exists a unique
\Phi^{h,abs}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z)\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\Phi^{K,abs}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z))
which solves (
7.3
). Moreover, we have

•

\Phi^{h,abs}
is smooth respect to
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.

•

\Phi^{h,abs}
is continuously Fréchet differentiable with respect to
h
,

•

\Phi^{h,abs}
has the same regularity as
h
seen as functions of
z
on
K
.

Remark 27
.

We could have applied the implicit function theorem to show the existence of solutions in a neighbourhood of each point of
Z^{K}
, but the neighbourhood will a priori depend on the point and on
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
. Hence, we used the fixed point theorem instead to obtain a uniform
\delta_{0}
, using the compactness of
\mathcal{B}_{bound}
.

Now, we choose
\delta_{0}
so that Lemma
48
,
49
and
50
are satisfied. It remains to show that
\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
,
\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}}
,
Z(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
consists of two connected components:
Z^{abs}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
and
Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
which is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
Now, we prove Proposition
20

Proof.

First, we apply lemmas
48
,
49
and
50
:
\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}\;,\;\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}
there exists a unique set of solutions
Z^{i}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
in the region
\mathscr{B}_{i}
given by

•

on every compact of
\mathscr{B}_{abs}
:

Z^{abs}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})=Graph\left(\Phi^{abs}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right),

•

on
\mathscr{B}_{1}
:

Z^{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})=Graph\left(\Phi^{h,1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right),

•

on
\mathscr{B}_{2}
:

Z^{2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})=Graph\left(\Phi^{h,2}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right),

•

on
\mathscr{B}_{3}
:

Z^{3}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})=Graph\left(\Phi^{h,3}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right),

•

on
\mathscr{B}_{4}
:

Z^{4}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})=Graph\left(\Phi^{h,4}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\right).

By uniqueness of solutions on
\mathscr{B}_{i}
, we obtain:

Z^{i}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})=Z^{j}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\quad\quad\quad\text{on}\quad\mathscr{B}_{i}\cap\mathscr{B}_{j}\quad\text{such that}\quad i\neq j.

(7.10)

We set

Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}):=\bigcup_{i=1\cdots 4}Z^{i}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}).

(7.11)

and

Z^{abs}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}):=Z^{abs}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}).

(7.12)

It is easy to see that

Z^{abs}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\cap Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})=\emptyset.

(7.13)

Therefore,
Z(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
has two connected components.
It remains to show that
Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. To see this, we construct a complete curve
\alpha:\mathbb{R}\to Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
which is periodic and which will parametrise
Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.

1.

there exist two unique points
(\rho_{i}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),0)\in Z^{i}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.

2.

We set

\alpha(0):=(\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),0)\quad\;\quad\alpha\left(\frac{1}{2}\right):=(\rho_{2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),0).

3.

Let
t_{0}<t_{1}\in\left]0,\frac{1}{2}\right[
. We set:

\rho_{0}:=\Phi^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\left(z^{K}_{min}-\frac{c}{2}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{1}:=\Phi^{3}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\left((z^{K}_{min}-\frac{c}{2}\right)

and

z_{i}:=z^{K}_{min}-\frac{c}{2}\quad\quad i\in\left\{0,1\right\}

so that

(\rho_{0},z_{0})\in Z^{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\cap Z^{3}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\quad\text{and}\quad(\rho_{1},z_{1})\in Z^{2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\cap Z^{3}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}).

4.

We construct the path
\alpha
on
]0,t_{0}[
by setting:

\alpha(t):=\left(\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z(t)),z(t):=z_{0}\frac{t}{t_{0}}\right).

5.

On
]t_{0},t_{1}[
,
\alpha
is defined by

\alpha(t):=\left(\rho(t):=\rho_{0}\frac{t_{1}-t}{t_{1}-t_{0}}+\rho_{1}\frac{t-t_{0}}{t_{1}-t_{0}},z(t):=\Phi^{3}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(\rho(t))\right).

6.

On
\left]t_{1},\frac{1}{2}\right[
,
\alpha
is defined by

\alpha(t):=\left(\Phi^{2}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(z(t)),z(t):=\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}-\frac{1}{2}}t-\frac{z_{1}}{2t_{1}-1}\right).

One can easily check that
\alpha
is continuous on
\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right]
. Now, by similar constructions, we extend continuously the construction to
\left[\frac{1}{2},1\right]
so that we obtain

\alpha(0)=\alpha(1).

7.

Note that the constructed curve is in fact
C^{1}
due to regularity of
\Phi^{i}
’s.

8.

Therefore,
\alpha
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

∎

Remark 28
.

We note that
\delta_{0}
depends on
\mathcal{B}_{bound}
and the parameters of the Kerr black hole
(a,M)
.

7.2
Further properties of the functions
\Phi^{i}

In order to introduce the dimensions of the matter cloud, we need the following lemma concerning the extrema of the functions
\Phi^{i}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(h,\cdot)
at a fixed
h
and
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})

Lemma 51
.

Let
\delta_{0}>0
be given by Proposition
20
. Then, after possibly shrinking
\delta_{0}
,
\forall h\in B(0,\delta_{0})
,
\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
, there exist

1.

a unique
z_{0}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in B_{\delta_{0}}(0)\subset]-\gamma,\gamma[
such that

\frac{\partial\Phi^{abs}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial z}(h,z_{0})=0.\;

Moreover,
\Phi^{abs}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot)
is maximal at this point.

2.

a unique
\rho_{max}^{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\rho_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))\subset I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
such that

\frac{\partial\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial\rho}(h,\rho_{max}^{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))=0\;,

Moreover,
\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot)
is maximal at this point.

3.

a unique
\rho_{max}^{2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\rho_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))\subset I^{2}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
such that

\frac{\partial\Phi^{2}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial\rho}(h,\rho_{max}^{2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))=0\;,

Moreover,
\Phi^{2}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot)
is minimal at this point.

4.

a unique
z_{c1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in B_{\delta_{0}}(0)\subset I^{3}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
such that

\frac{\partial\Phi^{3}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial z}(h,z_{c2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))=0\;,

Moreover,
\Phi^{3}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot)
is minimal at this point.

5.

a unique
z_{c2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in B_{\delta_{0}}(0)\subset I^{4}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
such that

\frac{\partial\Phi^{4}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial z}(h,z_{c2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))=0\;,

Moreover,
\Phi^{4}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot)
is maximal at this point.

Proof.

For example, in order to obtain the second point, we consider the mapping
\partial_{\rho}\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}:B_{\delta_{0}}(0)\times I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\frac{\partial\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial\rho}(h,\rho).

and we consider the equation

\frac{\partial\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial\rho}(h,\rho)=0.

By Lemma
44
, the point
(0,\rho_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))
is zero of the above equation. Moreover, by Lemma
48
,
\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}
is continuously Fréchet differentiable on its domain. Furthermore,

\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial\rho^{2}}(0,\rho_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))=\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi^{K,1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial\rho^{2}}(\rho_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))\neq 0.

Therefore, we apply the same method used in Lemma
48
(fixed point arguments) to obtain that there exists
\delta_{0}>0
independent of
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
(eventually smaller than the one chosen by Proposition
20
) such that
\displaystyle\forall h\in B_{\delta_{0}}(0)\subset\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}
there exists a unique
\rho_{max}^{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\rho_{max}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))\subset I^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}
such that

\frac{\partial\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}}{\partial\rho}(h,\rho_{max}^{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))=0\;,

Moreover,
\Phi^{1}_{\varepsilon,\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot)
is maximal at this point and
\rho_{max}^{1}(\cdot,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
is continuously Fréchet differentiable on
B_{\delta_{0}}(0)
.
∎

7.3
Localisation of the matter cloud

In this section, we will localise the matter cloud moving in the perturbed spacetime. More precisely, we will prove that for
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small, all trapped non-spherical timelike future-directed geodesics with
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
moving the perturbed spacetime are located inside
\mathscr{B}^{\pm,trapped}(a,M)
, defined in Proposition
17
. We will also construct a compact region of
\mathscr{B}^{\pm,trapped}(a,M)
which depends only on
\mathcal{B}_{bound}
and
(a,M)
which contains the support of the distribution function. Finally, we will arrange so that the domain of trapped non-spherical geodesics moving in the perturbed spacetime remains in
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}
.

First, we recall

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}},\rho^{2}+(z\pm\beta)^{2}>\frac{\beta}{a},|z|+|\rho|<\left(1+\frac{1}{b}\right)\beta\right\},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}},\rho^{2}+(z\pm\beta)^{2}>\frac{\beta}{a},|z|+|\rho|>\left(1-\frac{1}{b}\right)\beta\right\}.

We generalise Lemma
46
to the perturbed spacetimes in the following way.

Proposition 21
.

There exist
\;\delta_{0}>0\,,\,b>0
such that
\displaystyle\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
,
\displaystyle h\in B(0,\delta_{0})
we have

\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}\cap Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})=\emptyset\quad\text{and}\quad Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\subset\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}.

We set the quantities :

\displaystyle\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})

\displaystyle:=\min_{z\in]-z_{c}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),z_{c}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})[}\Phi^{1}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(z)\;,\;\rho_{2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}):=\max_{z\in]-z^{K}_{min},z^{K}_{min}[}\Phi^{2}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(z)\;,\;

(7.14)

\displaystyle z^{max}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})

\displaystyle:=\max_{]\rho_{c}^{1}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{c}^{2}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})[}\Phi^{3}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho)\;,\;z^{min}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}):=\min_{]\rho_{c}^{1}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{c}^{2}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})[}\Phi^{4}_{(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})}(\rho)

Now, we introduce the "dimensions of the matter shell" by defining:

\displaystyle\rho_{min}(h)

\displaystyle:=\min_{\mathcal{B}_{bound}}\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\;,\;\rho_{max}(h):=\max_{\mathcal{B}_{bound}}\rho_{2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\;,\;

\displaystyle Z_{max}(h)

\displaystyle:=\max_{\mathcal{B}_{bound}}z^{max}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\;,\;Z_{min}(h):=\min_{\mathcal{B}_{bound}}z^{min}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\;.\;

We give the proof of Proposition
21

Proof.

Let
\delta_{0}
be sufficiently small and let
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
. Let
(\rho,z)\in Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
. Then,
\rho\geq\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\geq\rho_{min}(h)
. Set

\rho_{min}(h)=\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0})\quad\text{for some}\quad(\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}.

By Lemma
46
,

\rho_{1}^{K}(\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0})>\left(1+\frac{1}{b}\right)\beta.

Recall that,

\forall(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{A}_{bound}\;,\;\rho_{1}^{K}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})>\rho^{mb}(a,M)>\left(1+\frac{1}{b}\right)\beta.

Therefore, there exists
r>0
uniform in
(\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0})
such that

B(\rho_{1}^{K}(\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0}),r)\subset\left]\left(1+\frac{1}{b}\right)\beta,\infty\right[.

From Proposition
20
, we have

\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0})\in B(\rho_{1}^{K}(\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0}),\delta_{0}).

Now, we adjust
\delta_{0}
so that

B(\rho_{1}^{K}(\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0}),\delta_{0})\subset B(\rho_{1}^{K}(\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0}),r)\subset\left]\left(1+\frac{1}{b}\right)\beta,\infty\right[.

Therefore,

\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon_{0},\ell_{0})\in\left]\left(1+\frac{1}{b}\right)\beta,\infty\right[.

Hence,
(\rho,z)\notin\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}
.
∎

Before we construct the compact support of the distribution function (for the spacetime variables), we will need the following lemma

Lemma 52
.

There exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that for all
h\in B(0,\delta_{0})
and for all
\displaystyle(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
we have

\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})-\rho_{0}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})>\eta,

where
\eta
is given by Lemma
40
.

Proof.

Let
\delta_{0}>0
be given by Proposition
20
. Let
h\in B(0,\delta_{0})
and
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
. Then, for sufficiently small
\delta_{0}
, we have

\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})>\rho^{K}_{1,min}-\frac{\eta}{2}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\rho_{0}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})<\rho^{K}_{0,max}+\frac{\eta}{2}.

Therefore,

\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})-\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})>\rho^{K}_{1,min}-\rho^{K}_{0,max}-\eta.

By (
4.156
), we obtain

\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})-\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})>2\eta-\eta=\eta.

∎

Finally, we obtain

Proposition 22
.

Assume that the distribution function
f
has the ansatz defined by (
5.2
). Then

\supp_{(\rho,z)}f\subset\subset\left[\rho_{min}(h),\rho_{max}(h)\right]\times\left[Z_{min}(h),Z_{max}(h)\right].

(7.15)

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle(\rho,z)\in\supp_{(\rho,z)}f
. Then,

(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\eta}\left(\rho-\rho_{1}(h,(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))\right)>0.

By (
3.23
) and since
(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}
,
(\rho,z)
must lie either in the region bounded by
\displaystyle Z^{trapped}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
or in the region bounded by
\displaystyle\partial(\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}\cup\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}})\bigcup Z^{abs}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
.

Since

\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\eta}\left(\rho-\rho_{1}(h,(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}))\right)>0,

by Lemma
52
, we have

\rho>\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})-\eta>\rho_{0}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}).

Thus,
(\rho,z)
cannot lie in the region bounded by
\displaystyle\partial(\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}\cup\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}})\bigcup Z^{abs}_{c}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})
. We conclude that

(\rho,z)\in\left[\rho_{1}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),\rho_{2}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\right]\times\left[z_{min}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),z_{max}(h,\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\right]\subset\left[\rho_{min}(h),\rho_{max}(h)\right]\times\left[Z_{min}(h),Z_{max}(h)\right].

This ends the proof.
∎

8
Set-up for solving the renormalised equations

8.1
Two fixed point lemmas

In this section, we state two variations of the classical fixed point theorem whose applications will allow us to solve the system of equations for the renormalised unknowns. These versions were derived and used in
[
17
]
.

Theorem 4

(Banach fixed point theorem)
.

Let
(X,d)
be a non-empty complete metric space with a contraction mapping
T:X\to X
. Then T admits a unique fixed point in X.

The following theorem is a consequence of
4
.

Theorem 5
.

Suppose that we have Banach spaces
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{Q}
, and
\mathcal{P}
,
\epsilon>0
and a map

\mathcal{T}:B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})\to B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}).

Furthermore, suppose that

1.

There exists a constant
D>0
such that
(l,q,p)\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
, we have

||\mathcal{T}(l,q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}\leq D(||l||^{2}_{\mathcal{L}}+||q||^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p||_{\mathcal{P}}).

2.

There exists a constant
D>0
such that
(l_{1},q_{1},p_{1}),(l_{2},q_{2},p_{2})\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
implies

\displaystyle||\mathcal{T}(l_{1},q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{T}(l_{2},q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\leq D\left[(||l_{1}||_{\mathcal{L}}+||l_{2}||_{\mathcal{L}})||l_{1}-l_{2}||+||q_{1}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}})||q_{1}-q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p_{1}-p_{2}||_{\mathcal{P}}\right].

Then after choosing
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, there exists a solution map
\mathcal{G}:B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})\to B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})
such that

1.

(q,p)\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
implies

\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G}(q,p),q,p)=\mathcal{G}(q,p).

2.

There exists a constant
D>0
such that
(q,p)\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
implies

||\mathcal{G}(q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}\leq D\left(||q||^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p||_{\mathcal{P}}\right).

3.

There exists a constant
D>0
such that
(q_{1},p_{1}),(q_{2},p_{2})\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
implies

||\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{G}(q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}\leq D\left((||q_{1}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}})||q_{1}-q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p_{1}-p_{2}||_{\mathcal{P}}\right).

Proof.

We apply Theorem
4
to

\mathcal{T}(.,q,p):B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\to B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L}).

For this we choose
\epsilon
sufficiently small so that
\mathcal{T}(.,p,q)
is a contraction map.

First we fix (q,p). By the second assumption, one has

\displaystyle||\mathcal{T}(l_{1},q,p)-\mathcal{T}(l_{2},q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\leq D\left((||l_{1}||_{\mathcal{L}}+||l_{2}||_{\mathcal{L}})||l_{1}-l_{2}||_{\mathcal{L}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2D\epsilon||l_{1}-l_{2}||_{\mathcal{L}}.

We choose
\epsilon
such that

2D\epsilon<1.

We apply Theorem
4
to have the first point of the theorem.

Now we derive the quadratic estimates,

\displaystyle||\mathcal{G}(q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=||\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G}(q,p),q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\leq D(||\mathcal{G}(q,p)||^{2}_{\mathcal{L}}+||q||^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}++||p||_{\mathcal{P}}),

\displaystyle\leq D\epsilon||\mathcal{G}(q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}+D(||q||^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p||_{\mathcal{P}}),

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}||\mathcal{G}(q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}+D(||q||^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p||_{\mathcal{P}}).

Hence

||\mathcal{G}(q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}\lesssim||q||^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p||_{\mathcal{P}}.

For the second quadratic estimates, we proceed in the same way:

\displaystyle||\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{G}(q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}=||\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1}),q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1}),q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}},

\displaystyle\leq D((||\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1})||_{\mathcal{L}}+||\mathcal{G}(q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}})||\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{G}(q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}+(||q_{1}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}})||q_{1}-q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p_{1}-p_{2}||_{\mathcal{P}})

\displaystyle\leq 2D\epsilon||\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{G}(q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}+D((||q_{1}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}})||q_{1}-q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p_{1}-p_{2}||_{\mathcal{P}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}||\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{G}(q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}+D((||q_{1}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}})||q_{1}-q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p_{1}-p_{2}||_{\mathcal{P}}).

Hence

||\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{G}(q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}\lesssim\left((||q_{1}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}})||q_{1}-q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p_{1}-p_{2}||_{\mathcal{P}}\right).

∎

As a consequence of Theorem
5
, we have the following version of the fixed point theorem

Theorem 6
.

Suppose we have a linear operator
L:\mathcal{L}\to\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
, an operator
N:\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P}\to\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
,
E:B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})\to\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
for some
\epsilon>0
, such that

1.

For all
(l,q,p)\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
, we have

E(l,q,p)=L(l)-N(l,q,p).

2.

We have a Banach space
\mathcal{N}\subset\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
and a bounded map
L^{-1}:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{L}
such that
H\in\mathcal{N}
implies

L(L^{-1}(H))=H.

3.

We have
N(B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P}))\subset\mathcal{N}
and there exists a constant
D>0
such that
(l,q,p)\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
implies

||N(l,q,p)||_{\mathcal{N}}\leq D(||l||^{2}_{\mathcal{L}}+||q||^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p||_{\mathcal{P}})

4.

There exists a constant
D>0
such that
(l_{1},q_{1},p_{1}),(l_{2},q_{2},p_{2})\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
implies

||N(l_{1},q_{1},p_{1})-N(l_{2},q_{2},p_{1})||_{\mathcal{N}}\leq D\left[(||l_{1}||_{\mathcal{L}}+||l_{2}||_{\mathcal{L}})||l_{1}-l_{2}||+||q_{1}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}})||q_{1}-q_{2}||+||p_{1}-p_{2}||_{\mathcal{P}}\right].

Then, after choosing a sufficiently small
\epsilon
, there exists a solution map
\mathcal{G}:B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})\to B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{L})
such that

1.

(q,p)\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
implies

E(\mathcal{G}(q,p),q,p)=0.

2.

There exists a constant
D>0
such that
(q,p)\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})\times B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
implies

||\mathcal{G}(q,p)||_{\mathcal{L}}\leq D\left(||q||^{2}_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p||_{\mathcal{P}}\right).

3.

There exists a constant
D>0
such that
\displaystyle q_{1},q_{2}\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q})
and
\displaystyle p_{1},p_{2}\in B_{\epsilon}(\mathcal{P})
imply

||\mathcal{G}(q_{1},p_{1})-\mathcal{G}(q_{2},p_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}\leq D\left[(||q_{1}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}})||q_{1}-q_{2}||_{\mathcal{Q}}+||p_{1}-p_{2}||_{\mathcal{P}}\right].

8.2
Toy Model

In this section, we present a model problem which indicates the general structure that we will exploit when we solve the equations for the renormalised unknowns.

Consider the following non-linear Poisson equation on the open unit ball of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with Dirichlet boundary condition:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta h&=N(h,\delta)(x)\quad\text{on}\;B_{1}\\
\left.h\right|_{\partial B_{1}}&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.1)

where
N\,:\,B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L})\times[0,\delta_{0}[\to\mathcal{L}
is the mapping defined by

N(h,\delta)(x):=\int_{K}\;F(x,v,\delta)\Psi(h(x),v)\,dv

where
K\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}:=C^{k+2,\alpha_{0}}(\overline{B_{1}})
with
\alpha_{0}\in(0,1)
,
k\geq 0
and
B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L})
is the open ball of
\mathcal{L}
of radius
\delta_{0}>0
centred at
0
.

We make the following assumptions on
F
:

•

\displaystyle F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times K\times[0,\delta_{0}[\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is continuous and
C^{k,\alpha_{0}}
with respect to the first variable,

•

\displaystyle\forall v\in K\;,\;\forall\delta\in[0,\delta_{0}[\;,\;F(\cdot,v,\delta)
is supported on
B_{1}
,

•

\displaystyle\forall x\in B_{1}\;,\;\forall v\in K\;,\;F(x,v,0)=0
,

•

\displaystyle\forall x\in B_{1}\;,\;\forall v\in K\;,\;F(x,v,\cdot)
is differentiable at
\delta=0
,

•

\displaystyle\Psi:\mathbb{R}\times K\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is smooth and compactly supported.

We have the trivial solution given by
h=0
with
\delta=0
. We will use the fixed point argument in order to construct a one-parameter family of solutions
(h(\delta))_{[0,\delta_{0}[}
of (
8.1
) which equals the trivial solution when
\delta=0
. We state the following result:

Proposition 23
.

There exists
\delta_{0}>0
sufficiently small such that there exists a solution map
h:[0,\delta_{0}[\to B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L})
such that

1.

\displaystyle\forall\delta\in[0,\delta_{0}[\;,\;h(\delta)
solves (
8.1
)
and

2.

there exists
C>0
such that
\forall\delta\in[0,\delta_{0}[
,

||h(\delta)||_{\mathcal{L}}\leq C\delta

3.

The one parameter family of solutions bifurcates from the trivial solution in the sense that
\delta\mapsto h(\delta)
is differentiable at
\delta=0
and
\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\,\delta^{-1}h(\delta)=\hat{h}
where
\hat{h}
is the solution of linear Poisson problem:

\left\{\begin{aligned} \Delta h&=\int_{K}\;\partial_{\delta}F(x,v,0)\Psi(0,v)\,dv\quad\text{on}\;B_{1}\\
\left.h\right|_{\partial B_{1}}&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(8.2)

Proof.

Let
\delta_{0}>0
. The proof relies on the application Theorem
6
with
L=\Delta:\mathcal{L}\to C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}})
and
N:\mathcal{L}\times\mathbb{R}\to C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}})
. To this end, we check the following assumptions:

1.

E:B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L})\times[0,\delta_{0}[\to C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}})
has the form

E(h,\delta)=\Delta(h)-N(h,\delta).

It is well defined by the dominated convergence theorem.

2.

We consider the linear Poisson equation with the previous Dirichlet boundary conditions :

\Delta h=H\quad\text{on }B_{1},\quad\quad h|_{\partial B_{1}}=0,

(8.3)

where
H
is compactly compactly supported on
B_{1}
and lies in
\mathcal{N}:=C^{k,\alpha_{0}}(\overline{B_{1}})
. Then, we can solve uniquely
22
22

22

We refer to
[
23
, Chapter 2.2]
for the derivation of the representation formula.
for
h\in C^{k+2,\alpha_{0}}(\overline{B_{1}})
:
More precisely,
h
is given by

\forall x\in B_{1}\;,\;h(x)=-\int_{B_{1}}\;G(x,y)H(y)dy

(8.4)

where
G
is the Green function for the unit ball. Now, the a priori estimates on
h
result from the potential estimates. More precisely, we apply Theorem
4.13
of
[
27
]
in order to obtain

h\in C^{k+2,\alpha_{0}}(\overline{B_{1}})\quad

and we have

||h||_{C^{k+2,\alpha_{0}}(\overline{B_{1}})}\leq C||H||_{\mathcal{N}}.

(8.5)

Therefore,
L^{-1}:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{L}
is well defined, it is bounded and we have

\forall H\in\mathcal{N}\;,\;L\left(L^{-1}(H)\right)=H.

3.

We need to check that

N\left(B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L})\times[0,\delta_{0}[\right)\subset\mathcal{N}

and that there exists
D>0
such that
(h,\delta)\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L})\times[0,\delta_{0}[
implies

||N(h,\delta)||_{\mathcal{N}}\leq D\left(||h||^{2}_{\mathcal{L}}+\delta\right).

(8.6)

We show the result for
k=0
. The general case is obtained using the same arguments.

•

Let
(h,\delta)\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L})\times[0,\delta_{0}[
. Then, by the regularity assumptions on
F
,
\forall v\in K
, the function
F(\cdot,v,\lx@nobreakspace\delta)\Psi(h(\cdot),v)
lies in
C^{0,\alpha_{0}}
. Since
\Psi
and
F(\cdot,\cdot,\delta)
are compactly supported, we apply the dominated convergence theorem. Therefore
N(h,\delta)
lies in
C^{0}(\overline{B}_{1})
.

•

It remains to show that
N(h,\delta)
is Hölder continuous. This follows using the Hölder regularity assumption for
F
and
h
and the dominated convergence theorem.

•

Now, we show the estimates (
8.6
). We claim that the mapping
N
is Fréchet differentiable at
(0,0)
.

Therefore, we have
\displaystyle\forall(h,\delta)\in B_{\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{L})\times[0,\delta_{0}[
with
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small, we have

N(h,\delta)=N(0,0)+D_{h}N(0,0)\cdot h+D_{\delta}N(0,0)\cdot\delta+O(||h||^{2}_{\mathcal{L}}+\delta^{2}).

By the assumptions on
F
, we have

N(0,0)=0\quad\text{and}\quad D_{h}N(0,0)=0.

(8.7)

Hence,

||N(h,\delta)||_{C^{0}(\overline{B}_{1})}\leq C(||h||^{2}_{\mathcal{L}}+\delta).

The Hölder part is estimated using similar arguments.

4.

The remaining step is to show that there exists
C>0
such that
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in[0,\delta_{0}[
implies

||h(\delta_{1})-h(\delta_{2})||_{\mathcal{L}}\leq C||\delta_{1}-\delta_{2}||.

∎

Now, we make an analogy with the reduced EV system:
h
corresponds to the renormalised quantities, the function
F
corresponds to the distribution function and
K
corresponds to
\mathcal{B}_{bound}
. Moreover, the compact support of
F
corresponds to the compact support of the matter terms. Finally, the ability to invert the Laplacian and solve for
h
corresponds to the use of the modified Carter-Robinson theory.

9
Solving for the renormalised quantities

9.1
Further analytical properties of the Kerr metric

As in
[
17
]
, We start by introducing the following function defined on
\mathscr{B}
by :

h(\rho,z):=\log\left(\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z-\beta)^{2}}-(z-\beta)\right)+\log\left(\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z+\beta)^{2}}+(z+\beta)\right).

(9.1)

This function will allow us to capture the singular behaviour of the Kerr metric coefficients. In fact, it behaves in the same way as
\log(X_{K})
near the horizon, the axis of symmetry, the poles and near at infinity. More precisely, we have

Lemma 53
.

Define a function

x_{K}(\rho,z):=\log(X_{K})-h.

Then,
x_{K}\in\hat{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathscr{B}})
.

Proof.

The proof is based on Taylor expansions of the different metric components around the singularities. First of all, note that away from the horizon, the axis of symmetry and the poles,
x_{k}
is smooth and all the derivatives are bounded.

•

Near the axis:

Define
\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{N}:=\left\{(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}\backslash\mathscr{A},\quad z>\beta\right\}
and let
(\rho,z)\in\mathscr{A}_{N}
.

Then,

h=2\log\rho+\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z+\beta)^{2}}+(z+\beta)}{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z-\beta)^{2}}+(z-\beta)}\right).

(9.2)

Thus,

\displaystyle x_{k}

\displaystyle=\log\left(\rho^{2}\frac{\Pi}{\Sigma^{2}\Delta}\right)-2\log\rho-\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z+\beta)^{2}}+(z+\beta)}{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z-\beta)^{2}}+(z-\beta)}\right),

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{\Pi}{\Sigma^{2}\Delta}\right)-\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z+\beta)^{2}}+(z+\beta)}{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z-\beta)^{2}}+(z-\beta)}\right).

Next note that
\displaystyle\frac{\Pi}{\Sigma^{2}\Delta}
is smooth on
\mathscr{A}_{N}
since both functions
\Sigma^{2}
and
\Delta
do not vanish on this set. Thus
x_{K}
is smooth around
\mathscr{A}_{N}
. In the same way
x_{K}
is smooth on
\mathscr{A}_{S}:=\left\{(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}\backslash\mathscr{A},\quad z<-\beta\right\}
.

•

Near the horizon:

Similarly, let
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}\subset\mathscr{H}
be a neighbourhood of the horizon. By the extendibility of Kerr around the horizon, we have

\log(X_{K})=\log(X_{\mathscr{H}}),

where
X_{\mathscr{H}}(0,z)>0
. Thus,
\log(X_{K})
is smooth near the horizon.

By Taylor expansion of
h
around the horizon, we find that the latter is also regular.

•

Near
p_{N}
:

Let
(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{N}
. We compute

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\log\left(\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z-\beta)^{2}}-(z-\beta)\right)+\log\left(\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z+\beta)^{2}}+(z+\beta)\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\sqrt{s^{2}\chi^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})\right)+\log\left(\sqrt{s^{2}\chi^{2}+(\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})+2\beta)^{2}}+(\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})+2\beta)\right).

The first term of the right hand side is given by

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle:=\log\left(\sqrt{s^{2}\chi^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(\chi^{2}+s^{2}-2s\chi)}-\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})\right),

\displaystyle=\log\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}(\chi^{2}+s^{2}+2s\chi)}-\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})\right),

\displaystyle=2\log s.

We compute the second term of the right hand side

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle:=\log\left(\sqrt{s^{2}\chi^{2}+\frac{1}{4}(\chi^{2}+s^{2}-2s^{2}\chi^{2})+\beta(\chi^{2}-s^{2})+2\beta^{2}}+(\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})+2\beta)\right),

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\chi^{2}+s^{2})^{2}+4\beta(\chi^{2}-s^{2})+4\beta^{2}}+(\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})+2\beta)\right).

II
is clearly smooth on
\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{N}
.
Now, we have

\displaystyle x_{k}(s,\chi)

\displaystyle=\log\left(\rho^{2}\frac{\Pi}{\Sigma^{2}\Delta}\right)-h,

\displaystyle=2\log s+\log\frac{\chi^{2}}{\Delta}+\log\frac{\Pi}{\Sigma^{2}}-h,

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\chi^{2}}{\Delta}+\log\frac{\Pi}{\Sigma^{2}}-\log\left(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\chi^{2}+s^{2})^{2}+4\beta(\chi^{2}-s^{2})+4\beta^{2}}+(\frac{1}{2}(\chi^{2}-s^{2})+2\beta)\right).

\Delta
vanishes at
p_{N}
. We make a Taylor expansion for
\log\frac{\chi^{2}}{\Delta}
around
p_{N}
:

\tilde{r}=\tilde{r}_{+}+\chi^{2}\tilde{r}_{N}(s,\chi),

where
\tilde{r}_{N}
is smooth and does not vanish on
\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{N}
. It is given by

\tilde{r}_{N}(s,\chi):=\frac{2\beta}{4\beta-s^{2}}+O(s^{2}\chi^{2}).

Moreover,

\Delta=(\tilde{r}-\tilde{r}_{+})(\tilde{r}-\tilde{r}_{-}).

Hence,

\log\frac{\chi^{2}}{\Delta}=\log\frac{1}{\tilde{r}_{N}(\tilde{r}-\tilde{r}_{-})}.

The latter is smooth on
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{N}}
. The same arguments are applied near
p_{S}
.
∎

Now, we recall from
[
17
]
the following decay estimates for
Y_{K}
, the Ernst potential defined by (
5.33
):

Lemma 54
.

On
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}\cup\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}
, we have

\frac{\rho}{X^{2}_{K}}|\partial Y_{K}|\leq Cr^{-4},\quad|\partial\left(X^{-1}_{K}\partial Y_{K}\right)|\leq Cr^{-4},\quad|\partial^{2}\left(X^{-1}_{K}\partial Y_{K}\right)|\leq Cr^{-5}.

We have the stronger bound for the
z-
component of the derivative of
Y_{K}
on
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}\cup\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}
,

\frac{1}{X^{2}_{K}}|\partial_{z}Y_{K}|\leq Cr^{-5},\quad|\partial\left(X^{-2}_{K}\partial Y_{K}\right)|\leq Cr^{-6},\quad|\partial^{2}\left(X^{-2}_{K}\partial Y_{K}\right)|\leq Cr^{-7}.

On
\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{N}
we have the estimates

|\underline{\partial}Y_{K}|\leq Cs^{3},\quad|\underline{\partial}^{2}Y_{K}|\leq Cs^{2},\quad|\underline{\partial}^{3}Y_{K}|\leq Cs.

The latter estimates remain valid on
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{S}}
.
Here,
r
is defined by:

r:=\sqrt{1+\rho^{2}+z^{2}}.

Lemma 55
.

We have

•

\frac{|\partial X_{K}|}{X_{K}}\sim|\partial h|\quad\text{near}\;\mathscr{H},\mathscr{A},p_{N},p_{S}.

•

There exist
c,C>0
such that
\forall(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}}
, we have

ce^{h}\left|\partial h\right|\leq\left|\partial X_{K}\right|\leq Ce^{h}\left|\partial h\right|.

Proof.

1.

We prove the equivalence in every region:
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}
,
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}
,
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{N}}
and
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{S}}
.

(a)

Near
\mathscr{H}
: We have

\displaystyle\partial\log X_{K}

\displaystyle=\partial(\log X_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{X_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z)}\left(2\rho\partial_{\rho}X_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z)\;\;\partial_{z}X_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z)\right)^{t}

and

\partial h(\rho,z)=\left(2\rho\partial_{\rho}\overline{h}(\rho^{2},z)\;\;-\frac{2z}{\beta^{2}-z^{2}}+\partial_{z}\overline{h}(\rho^{2},z)\right)^{t}

where
\overline{h}
is some smooth function defined on
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}
. Recall that,
X_{\mathscr{H}}
is given by

X_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z)=\frac{\beta^{2}-z^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\frac{4r_{H}^{2}}{\Sigma^{2}(\rho^{2},z)}+\overline{X}_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z)

where
\overline{X}_{\mathscr{H}}
is some smooth function defined on
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}
. Now, we compute:

\displaystyle\partial_{z}X_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z)

\displaystyle=\partial_{z}\left(\frac{\beta^{2}-z^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\frac{4r_{H}^{2}}{\Sigma^{2}(\rho^{2},z)}-4r_{H}^{2}\frac{\beta^{2}-z^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\frac{\partial_{z}\Sigma^{2}(\rho^{2},z)}{\Sigma^{4}(\rho^{2},z)}+\partial_{z}X_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z).

On the horizon, we have

\partial_{z}X_{\mathscr{H}}(0,z)=0\;,\;\overline{X}_{\mathscr{H}}(\rho^{2},z)=0\;,\;\partial_{z}\overline{h}(0,z)=0\;,\;\Sigma^{2}(0,z)=r_{H}^{2}+\frac{z^{2}}{\beta^{2}}.

Now, straightforward computations imply:

\frac{\partial_{z}X_{\mathscr{H}}(0,z)}{X_{\mathscr{H}}(0,z)}=-\frac{4r_{H}z}{\beta^{2}-z^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{z}h(0,z)=-\frac{2z}{\beta^{2}-z^{2}}.

Therefore,

\lim_{\rho\to 0;|z|<\beta}\,\frac{|\partial X_{K}|}{X_{K}|\partial h|}=4r_{H}^{2}.

(b)

Near
\mathscr{A}
: We have

\displaystyle\partial\log X_{K}

\displaystyle=2\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\;\;0\right)^{t}+\partial f_{\mathscr{A}},

\displaystyle\partial h

\displaystyle=2\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\;\;0\right)^{t}+\partial g_{\mathscr{A}},

where
f_{\mathscr{A}},g_{\mathscr{A}}\in\hat{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}})
with bounded derivatives. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{|\partial\log X_{K}|^{2}}{|\partial h|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\frac{4}{\rho^{2}}+\frac{4}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}f+|\partial f|^{2}}{\frac{4}{\rho^{2}}+\frac{4}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g+|\partial g|^{2}}.

The latter goes to
1
when
\rho\to 0
.

(c)

Near
p_{N}
and
p_{S}
:

\displaystyle\log X_{K}

\displaystyle=2\log s+f_{N}(s,\chi)

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=2\log s+g_{N}(s,\chi)

where
f_{N},g_{N}\in\hat{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathscr{B}_{N}})
. Hence,

\frac{|\partial X_{K}|^{2}}{X_{K}^{2}|\partial h|^{2}}=\frac{|\underline{\partial}X_{K}|^{2}}{X_{K}^{2}|\underline{\partial}h|^{2}}=\frac{\left(\frac{2}{s}+\partial_{s}f_{N}\right)^{2}+(\partial_{\chi}f_{N})^{2}}{\left(\frac{2}{s}+\partial_{s}g_{N}\right)^{2}+(\partial_{\chi}g_{N})^{2}}.

The latter goes to
1
when
(s,\chi)\to(0,0)
.

2.

Now, we prove that there exist
c,C>0
such that
\forall(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}}
, we have

ce^{h}\left|\partial h\right|\leq\left|\partial X_{K}\right|\leq Ce^{h}\left|\partial h\right|.

We write
\forall(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}}
:

\frac{|\partial X_{K}|}{e^{h}|\partial h|}=\frac{|\partial X_{K}|}{X_{K}|\partial h|}X_{K}e^{-h}=e^{x_{K}}\frac{|\partial X_{K}|}{X_{K}|\partial h|}.

By Lemma
53
,
x_{K}\in\hat{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathscr{B}})
. Moreover, we have

\frac{|\partial X_{K}|}{X_{K}}\sim|\partial h|\quad\text{near}\;\mathscr{H},\mathscr{A},p_{N},p_{S}

by the first point. Hence

|\partial X_{K}|\sim e^{h}|\partial h|\quad\text{on}\quad\overline{\mathscr{B}}.

∎

Lemma 56
.

There exist smooth vector fields
e_{A}
,
e_{N}
and
e_{S}
defined on
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}
,
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{N}}
and
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{S}}
respectively, which all extend to smooth vector fields on
\overline{\mathscr{B}}
, such that

\partial h_{\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}\cap\mathscr{A}}=\frac{2}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}+e_{A},\quad\underline{\partial}h_{\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{N}}=\frac{2}{s}\underline{\partial}_{s}+e_{s},\quad\underline{\partial}h_{\overline{\mathscr{B}}_{S}}=\frac{2}{s^{\prime}}\underline{\partial}_{s}^{\prime}+e_{s},

Moreover,
e_{A}
verifies the decay estimate at infinity:

\left|e_{A}\right|=O_{r\to+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right).

(9.3)

Proof.

1.

Let
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\subset\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}
be a neighbourhood of the axis and let
(\rho,z)\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
. Then

\displaystyle\left.\partial\right|_{\tilde{\mathscr{A}}}

\displaystyle=2\left(\frac{1}{\rho}\;\;0\right)^{t}+\partial g_{\mathscr{A}}

\displaystyle=2\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}+e_{A},

where
e_{A}:=\partial g_{\mathscr{A}}\in\hat{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}})
and which can be extended smoothly to
\overline{\mathscr{B}}
.

By (
9.2
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial g_{\mathscr{A}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\partial\left(\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z+\beta)^{2}}+(z+\beta)}{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z-\beta)^{2}}+(z-\beta)}\right)\right)\right|^{2}.

Set

P_{\pm}(\rho,z):=\sqrt{\rho^{2}+(z\pm\beta)^{2}}+(z\pm\beta).

Then

\displaystyle\left|\partial g_{\mathscr{A}}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{P^{2}_{-}(\rho,z)}{P^{2}_{+}(\rho,z)}\frac{1}{P^{4}_{-}(\rho,z)}\left(|\partial P_{+}(\rho,z)|^{2}P^{2}_{-}(\rho,z)+|\partial P_{-}(\rho,z)|^{2}P^{2}_{+}(\rho,z)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-2P_{+}(\rho,z)P_{-}(\rho,z)\partial P_{+}(\rho,z)\cdot\partial P_{-}(\rho,z)\right).

When
r=\sqrt{1+\rho^{2}+z^{2}}\to\infty
, we have

\frac{P^{2}_{-}(\rho,z)}{P^{2}_{+}(\rho,z)}=O(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{P^{4}_{-}(\rho,z)}=O(r^{-4}).

Moreover,

|\partial P_{\pm}(\rho,z)|^{2}=\left(\frac{\rho}{\rho^{2}+(z\pm\beta)^{2}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{z}{\rho^{2}+(z\pm\beta)^{2}}+1\right)^{2}=O(r^{-2})

and

\left|\partial P_{+}(\rho,z)\cdot\partial P_{-}(\rho,z)\right|=O(r^{-2}).

Therefore,

\left|\partial g_{\mathscr{A}}\right|=O_{r\to\infty}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\right).

2.

The expansion near
p_{N}
and near
p_{S}
is obtained in the same manner.

∎

9.2
Regularity of the matter terms
F_{i}

First of all, we recall that
F_{i}
are given by

F_{1}(W,X,\sigma)(\rho,z):=-e^{2\lambda}\frac{2\pi\rho}{\sigma}\int_{D(\rho,z)}(X+2(\rho L)^{2})\Phi(E+\rho\omega L,\rho L)\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(E+\rho\omega L,\rho L),(X,W,\sigma))\,dEdL,

(9.4)

F_{2}(W,X,\sigma)(\rho,z):=\frac{2\pi\rho}{\sigma}X\int_{D(\rho,z)}\rho LE\Phi(E+\rho\omega L,\rho L)\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(E+\rho\omega L,\rho L),(X,W,\sigma))\,dEdL,

(9.5)

\displaystyle F_{3}(W,X,\sigma)(\rho,z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2\pi\rho^{3}\sigma}{X^{2}}\int_{D(\rho,z)}\left(\tilde{L}^{2}-L^{2}\right)\Phi(E+\rho\omega L,\rho L)\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(E+\rho\omega L,\rho L),(X,W,\sigma))\,dEdL,

(9.6)

\displaystyle F_{4}(W,X,\sigma,\lambda)(\rho,z):=-\frac{4\pi e^{2\lambda}\rho}{\sigma}

\displaystyle\int_{D(\rho,z)}\left(\frac{X^{2}}{\rho^{2}\sigma^{2}}E^{2}+\left(1-\frac{X}{\rho^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\rho^{2}}{X}L^{2}\right)\right)\Phi(E+\rho\omega L,\rho L)

(9.7)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(E+\rho\omega L,\rho L),(X,W,\sigma))\,dEdL,

Lemma 57
.

Let
\delta_{0}>0
be given by Proposition
20
and let
h:=\left(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{\sigma},\overset{\circ}{X}\right)\in B_{\delta_{0}}\subset\mathcal{L}_{\Theta}\times\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\times\mathcal{L}_{X}
. Then, the matter terms
F_{i}(h)
as defined in (
9.4
)-(
9.7
) are well-defined on
\overline{\mathscr{B}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
22
, we have

\left(\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}\cup\overline{\mathscr{B}_{N}}\cup\overline{\mathscr{B}_{S}}\right)\cap\supp_{(\rho,z)}f=\emptyset

and

\supp_{(\rho,z)}f\subset\subset\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}.

Now let
(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}
. If
(\rho,z)\in\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
where
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
is some open neighbourhood of the axis, then by Proposition
4
, there exists a smooth function
X_{\mathscr{A}}:\tilde{\mathscr{A}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
such that
\displaystyle X_{\mathscr{A}}(0,z)>0
and

X_{K}(\rho,z)=\rho^{2}X_{\mathscr{A}}(\rho^{2},z).

(9.8)

Hence, near the axis,
\tilde{L}
is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(E,\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma},\rho,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{X_{K}}}{\rho}\sqrt{1+\overset{\circ}{X}}\left(-1+\frac{X_{K}}{\rho^{2}}\frac{1+\overset{\circ}{X}}{(1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma})^{2}}E^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{X_{\mathscr{A}}}\sqrt{1+\overset{\circ}{X}}\left(-1+X_{\mathscr{A}}\frac{1+\overset{\circ}{X}}{(1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma})^{2}}E^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

which is well defined on
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
. The term
\displaystyle\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{X}}
is also well defined on
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
since

\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{X}}=\frac{\rho(1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma})}{\sqrt{X_{K}(1+\overset{\circ}{X})}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{X_{\mathscr{A}}}}\frac{1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{1+\overset{\circ}{X}}}.

Therefore, the matter terms
F_{i}(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma})
are well defined on
\tilde{\mathscr{A}}
, thus on
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}
.

Now, if
(\rho,z)\in\left(\overline{\mathscr{B}_{H}}\cup\overline{\mathscr{B}_{N}}\cup\overline{\mathscr{B}_{S}}\right)
, then
F_{i}(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma})
vanish. Hence, they are well defined.
∎

9.2.1
Further computations of the matter terms
F_{i}

In this section, we compute explicitly the intersection of
D(\rho,z)
with
\supp\Phi
. To this end, we will have to distinguish between direct and retrograde orbits. In this case, we write

f(x,v)=\left(\Phi_{+}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})+\Phi_{-}(\varepsilon,\ell_{z})\right)\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(\varepsilon,\ell_{z}),h)

(9.9)

where
\Phi_{+}
is supported on
\mathcal{B}_{bound}^{+}
and
\Phi_{-}
is supported on
\mathcal{B}_{bound}^{-}
.

Let
(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}
. Recall the definition of
D(\rho,z)
:

D(\rho,z)=\left\{(E,L)\;:\;E\geq\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{X}}\quad\text{and }\quad|L|\leq\tilde{L}(E,X,\sigma,\rho,z)\right\}

where

\tilde{L}(E,X,\sigma,\rho,z):=\frac{\sqrt{X}}{\rho}\left(-1+\frac{X}{\sigma^{2}}E^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(9.10)

Now, let
(E,L)\in D(\rho,z)
. Then,

E\geq\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{X}}\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq\frac{\rho^{2}}{X}L^{2}\leq\frac{X}{\sigma^{2}}E^{2}-1.

(9.11)

This is equivalent to

E\geq\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{X}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\sigma^{2}}{X}\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{X}\left(\frac{\rho^{2}}{X}L^{2}+1\right)\leq E^{2}.

(9.12)

Thus,

E\geq\tilde{E}(L,X,\sigma,\rho,z)

where
\tilde{E}
is defined by

\tilde{E}(L,X,\sigma,\rho,z):=\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{X}}\sqrt{1+\frac{\rho^{2}}{X}L^{2}}.

(9.13)

Now, we introduce the following change of variables

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle:=E+\rho\omega s,

(9.14)

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=L.

Therefore
23
23

23

Recall that
E_{\ell_{z}}(h,\cdot,\cdot)
is the effective potential energy of a particle with angular momentum
\ell_{z}
defined by (
7.2
).
,

D(\rho,z)=\left\{(\xi,s)\;:\;s\in\mathbb{R}\quad\text{and }\quad\xi\geq\tilde{E}(s,X,\sigma,\rho,z)+\rho\omega s=E_{\rho s}((\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma}),\rho,z)\right\}

and the matter terms are given by

F_{1}(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma})(\rho,z)=-\frac{2\pi e^{2\left(\overset{\circ}{\lambda}+\lambda_{K}\right)}}{1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma}}\int_{D(\rho,z)}(X_{K}(1+\overset{\circ}{X})+2(\rho s)^{2})\Phi(\xi,\rho s)\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(\xi,\rho s),(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma}))\,d\xi ds,

F_{2}(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma})=\frac{2\pi X_{K}(1+\overset{\circ}{X})}{1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma}}\int_{D(\rho,z)}\rho s(\xi-\rho\left(-\overset{\circ}{\Theta}+\omega_{K}\right)s)\Phi(\xi,\rho s)\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(\xi,\rho s),(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma}))\,d\xi ds,

\displaystyle F_{3}(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma})(\rho,z)=\frac{\rho^{4}}{X_{K}^{2}}\frac{2\pi(1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma})}{(1+\overset{\circ}{X})^{2}}

\displaystyle\int_{D(\rho,z)}\left(\tilde{L}^{2}\left(\xi-\rho\left(-\overset{\circ}{\Theta}+\omega_{K}\right)s,X_{K}(1+\overset{\circ}{X}),\sigma,\rho,z\right)-s^{2}\right)\Phi(\xi,\rho s)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(\xi,\rho s),(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma}))\,d\xi ds,

\displaystyle F_{4}(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma})(\rho,z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi e^{2\left(\overset{\circ}{\lambda}+\lambda_{K}\right)}}{1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma}}\int_{D(\rho,z)}\left(\frac{X_{K}^{2}(1+\overset{\circ}{X})^{2}}{\rho^{4}\left(1+\overset{\circ}{\sigma}\right)^{2}}\left(\xi-\rho\left(-\overset{\circ}{\Theta}+\omega_{K}\right)s\right)^{2}\right.+

\displaystyle\left.\left(1-\frac{X_{K}(1+\overset{\circ}{X})}{\rho^{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\rho^{2}}{X_{K}(1+\overset{\circ}{X})}s^{2}\right)\right)\Phi(\xi,\rho s)\Psi_{\eta}(\rho,(\xi,\rho s),(\overset{\circ}{\Theta},\overset{\circ}{X},\overset{\circ}{\sigma}))\,d\xi ds.

Now, we compute
\displaystyle D(\rho,z)\cap\supp\Phi(\cdot,\rho\cdot)
provided
(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}}
.
Since the matter shell contains retrograde and direct orbits, we introduce

D^{+}(\rho,z)=\left\{(\xi,s)\in D(\rho,z)\;:\;\omega s\geq 0\right\},

(9.15)

D^{-}(\rho,z)=\left\{(\xi,s)\in D(\rho,z)\;:\;\omega s\leq 0\right\},

(9.16)

Hence,

D(\rho,z)\cap\supp\Phi(\cdot,\rho\cdot)=\left(D^{+}(\rho,z)\cap\mathcal{B}_{bound}^{+}\right)\cup\left(D^{-}(\rho,z)\cap\mathcal{B}_{bound}^{-}\right)

We state the following result:

Lemma 58
.

Let
(\rho,z)\in\overline{\mathscr{B}_{A}}
. Then,

•

if
\rho=0
, then

D(\rho,z)\cap\supp\Phi(\cdot,\rho\cdot)=\emptyset.

(9.17)

•

Otherwise,

\displaystyle\supp\Phi_{+}(\cdot,\rho\cdot)\cap D^{+}(\rho,z)

\displaystyle\subset\left\{(\xi,s)\;:\;E_{\rho s}(h,\rho,z)\leq\xi\leq\varepsilon^{+}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{L}(\varepsilon^{+}_{2},h,\rho,z)\geq s\geq\frac{\ell_{1}^{+}}{\rho}\right\},

\displaystyle\supp\Phi_{-}(\cdot,\rho\cdot)\cap D^{-}(\rho,z)

\displaystyle\subset\left\{(\xi,s)\;:\;E_{\rho s}(h,\rho,z)\leq\xi\leq\varepsilon^{-}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad-\tilde{L}(\varepsilon^{-}_{2}-\omega\ell_{1}^{-},h,\rho,z)\leq s\leq\frac{\ell_{2}^{-}}{\rho}\right\},

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle(\xi,s)\in D(\rho,z)\cap\supp\Phi(\cdot,\rho\cdot)
. Then,

\xi\geq E_{\rho s}(h,(\rho,z))\quad\text{and}\quad(\xi,\rho s)\in\mathcal{B}_{bound}.

•

If
\rho=0
. Then,
(\xi,\rho s)\notin\mathcal{B}_{bound}
. Hence,
\displaystyle D(\rho,z)\cap\supp\Phi(\cdot,\rho\cdot)=\emptyset
.

•

Otherwise,

1.

If
\displaystyle(\xi,s)\in D^{+}(\rho,z)\cap\supp\Phi_{+}(\cdot,\rho\cdot)
, then

\displaystyle\omega s\geq 0\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\xi\geq E_{\rho s}(h,\rho,z),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}^{+}\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{2}^{+}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\frac{\ell_{1}^{+}}{\rho}\leq s\leq\frac{\ell_{2}^{+}}{\rho}.

This implies

E_{\rho s}(h,\rho,z)=\tilde{E}(s,h,\rho,z)+\omega\rho s\leq\varepsilon_{2}^{+}.

Since
\omega s\geq 0
, we obtain

\tilde{E}(s,h,\rho,z)\leq\varepsilon_{2}^{+}.

Since
\tilde{E}
is monotonically increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with respect to
s
, we obtain a upper bound on
s
:

s\leq\tilde{L}(\varepsilon_{2}^{+},h,\rho,z).

Finally, we recall that we also have an upper bound on
s
, given by
\displaystyle\frac{\ell_{1}}{\rho}
, which provides the desired result.

2.

Similarly, if
\displaystyle(\xi,s)\in D^{-}(\rho,z)\cap\supp\Phi_{-}(\cdot,\rho\cdot)
, then,

\displaystyle\omega s\leq 0\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\xi\geq E_{\rho s}(h,\rho,z),

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}^{-}\leq\xi\leq\varepsilon_{2}^{-}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\frac{\ell_{1}^{-}}{\rho}\leq s\leq\frac{\ell_{2}^{-}}{\rho}.

This implies,
